Local Well-posedness for the Motion of a Compressible Gravity Water Wave with Vorticity
Chenyun Luo, Junyan Zhang

Abstract
In this paper we prove the local well-posedness (LWP) for the 3D compressible Euler equations describing the motion of a liquid in an unbounded initial domain with moving boundary. The liquid is under the influence of gravity but without surface tension, and it is not assumed to be irrotational. We apply the tangential smoothing method introduced in Coutand-Shkoller [ 10 , 11 ] to construct the approximation system with energy estimates uniform in the smooth parameter. It should be emphasized that, when doing the nonlinear a priori estimates, we need neither the higher order wave equation of the pressure and delicate elliptic estimates, nor the higher regularity on the flow-map or initial vorticity. Instead, we adapt the Alinhac’s good unknowns to the estimates of full spatial derivatives.



1
Introduction

In this paper we study the motion of a compressible gravity water wave in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
described by the compressible Euler equations:

\begin{cases}\rho D_{t}u=-\nabla p-\rho ge_{3},&~~~\text{in}\,\mathcal{D}\\
D_{t}\rho+\rho\text{div }u=0&~~~\text{in}\,\mathcal{D}\\
p=p(\rho)&~~~\text{in}\,\mathcal{D}\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{0\leq t\leq T}\{t\}\times\mathcal{D}_{t}
with
\mathcal{D}_{t}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x_{3}\leq S(t,x_{1},x_{2})\}
representing the unbounded domain occupied by the fluid at each fixed time
t
, whose boundary
\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}):x_{3}=S(t,x_{1},x_{2})\}
moves with the velocity of the fluid.
\nabla:=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{3}})
is the standard spatial derivative and
\text{div }X:=\nabla\cdot X
is the divergence for any vector field
X
in
\mathcal{D}_{t}
.
D_{t}:=\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla
denotes the material derivative. In (
1.1
),
u,\rho,p
represent the fluid velocity, density and pressure, respectively, and
g>0
is the gravity constant. The third equation of (
1.1
) is known to be the equation of states which satisfies

\displaystyle p^{\prime}(\rho)>0,\quad\text{for}\,\,\rho\geq\bar{\rho}_{0},

(1.2)

where
\bar{\rho}_{0}:=\rho|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}
is a positive constant (we set
{\bar{\rho}}_{0}=1
for simplicity), which is in the case of an isentropic liquid. The equation of states is required in order to close the system of compressible Euler equations.

The initial and boundary conditions of the system (
1.1
) are

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}=\{x:(0,x)\in\mathcal{D}\},\quad\text{and}\,\,u=u_{0},\rho=\rho_{0}~~~\text{on}\,\{0\}\times\mathcal{D}_{0},

(1.3)

\displaystyle D_{t}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}\in T(\partial\mathcal{D})\quad\text{and}\,\,p|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}=0,

(1.4)

where
T(\partial\mathcal{D})
stands for the tangent bundle of
\partial\mathcal{D}
. The first condition in (
1.4
) means the boundary moves with the velocity of the fluid, and the second one shows that outside the fluid region
\mathcal{D}_{t}
is the vacuum and the surface tension is neglected.

Energy conservation law

The system of equations (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) admits a conserved energy. Let

\displaystyle E_{0}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho|u|^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho Q(\rho)\,dx+\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}\cap\{x_{3}>0\}}gx_{3}\,dx-\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}^{c}\cap\{x_{3}<0\}}gx_{3}\,dx+\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}g(\rho-1)x_{3}\,dx

(1.5)

where
Q(\rho)=\int_{1}^{\rho}p(\lambda)\lambda^{-2}\,d\lambda
and
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{c}
denotes the complement of
\mathcal{D}_{t}
. A direct computation (cf.
[
42
, Section 1.1]
) yields that
\frac{d}{dt}E_{0}(t)=0
.

Enthalpy formulation and Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition

We introduce the new variable
h=h(\rho):=\int_{1}^{\rho}p^{\prime}(\lambda)\lambda^{-1}\,d\lambda
, which is known to be the enthalpy of the fluid. It can be seen that
h^{\prime}(\rho)>0
and
h|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}=0
thanks to (
1.2
). Since
\rho
can then be thought as a function of
h
, we define
e(h):=\log\rho(h)
. Under these new variables, (
1.1
) and (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{cases}D_{t}u=-\nabla h-ge_{3},&~~~\text{in}\,\mathcal{D},\\
\text{div}\,u=-D_{t}e(h),&~~~\text{in}\,\mathcal{D},\\
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\{x:(0,x)\in\mathcal{D}\},\\
u=u_{0},h=h_{0}&~~~\text{on}\,\{0\}\times\mathcal{D}_{0},\\
D_{t}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}\in T(\partial\mathcal{D})\quad\text{and}\,\,h=0&~~~\text{on}\,\partial\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

(1.6)

The system (
1.6
) looks exactly like the incompressible Euler equations, where
h
takes the position of
p
but
\text{div}\,u
is no longer
0
and determined as a function of
\rho
(and hence
h
). In addition, in Ebin
[
16
]
, the free-boundary problem (
1.6
) is known to be ill-posed unless the physical sign condition (also known as the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition)

\displaystyle-\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}h\geq c_{0}>0,\quad\text{on}\,\,\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}.

(1.7)

holds. Here,
\mathcal{N}
is the outward unit normal of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}
and
\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}:=\mathcal{N}\cdot\nabla
. The condition (
1.7
) is a natural physical condition which says that the enthalpy and hence the pressure and density is larger in the interior than on the boundary. We remark here that (
1.7
) can be derived by the strong maximum principle if the water wave is assumed to be irrotational
[
62
,
63
,
42
]
, and the existence of the positive constant
c_{0}
is a consequence of the presence of the gravity. Otherwise, we have merely that
-\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}h>0
, which is insufficient to close the a priori energy estimate for (
1.6
).

Equation of state for an isentropic liquid

We would like to impose the following natural conditions on
e(h)
: For each fixed
k\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C>1
such that

\displaystyle C^{-1}\leq|e^{(k)}(h)|\leq C.

(1.8)

In fact, (
1.8
) holds true if the equation of states is given by

\displaystyle p(\rho)=\gamma^{-1}(\rho^{\gamma}-1),\quad\gamma\geq 1.

(1.9)

In particular, when
\gamma=1
, a direct computation yields that
e(h)=h
.

Compatibility conditions on initial data

Finally, in order for the initial boundary value problem (
1.6
)-(
1.7
) to be solvable the initial data has to satisfy certain compatibility conditions at the boundary. In particular
h
verifies a wave equation by taking divergence to the first equation of (
1.6
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} D_{t}^{2}e(h)-\Delta h=(\nabla_{\mu}u^{\nu})(\nabla_{\nu}u^{\mu}),\quad&\text{in}\,\,\,\mathcal{D},\\
h=0,\quad&\text{on}\,\,\,\partial\mathcal{D},\\
h|_{t=0}=h_{0},\quad D_{t}h|_{t=0}=h_{1},\quad&\text{in }\{t=0\}\times\mathcal{D}_{0},\end{aligned}

(1.10)

where
h_{1}
can be determined in terms of
u_{0}
and
h_{0}
via the second equation of (
1.6
), i.e.,
-e^{\prime}(h_{0})h_{1}=\text{div}\,u_{0}
. In above and throughout, the summation convention is used for repeated upper and lower indices, we adopt the convention that
the Greek indices range over
1,2,3
, and the Latin indices range over
1
and
2
.
The compatibility conditions must be satisfied in order for (
1.10
) to have a sufficiently regular solution.

Since
h|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}=0
and
D_{t}\in T(\partial\mathcal{D})
, the second equation of (
1.6
) implies that
\text{div}\,u|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}=0
. We must therefore have
h_{0}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{0}}=0
and
\text{div}\,u_{0}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{0}}=0
, which is the zero-th compatibility condition. In general, for each
k\geq 0
, the
k
-th order compatibility condition reads

\displaystyle D_{t}^{j}h|_{\{0\}\times\partial\mathcal{D}_{0}}=0,\quad j=0,1,\cdots,k.

(1.11)

In
[
42
, Sect. 7]
, we have proved that for each fixed
k\geq 0
, there exists initial data verifying the compatibility condition up to order
k
such that the initial energy norm is bounded.

1.1
History and Background

The study of the motion of a fluid has a long history in mathematics, and the study of the free-boundary problems has blossomed over the past two decades or so. However, much of this activity has focus on incompressible fluid models, i.e., the velocity vector field satisfies
\text{div}\,u=0
and the density
\rho
is fixed to be a constant. Also, the pressure
p
is not determined by the equation of states. Rather, it is a Lagrange multiplier enforcing the divergence free constraint. It is worth mentioning here that when the fluid domain is unbounded and the velocity
u_{0}
is irrotational (i.e.,
\text{curl }u_{0}=0
, a condition that preserved by the evolution), this problem is called the (incompressible) water wave problem, which has received a great deal of attention. The local well-posedness (LWP) for the free-boundary incompressible Euler equations in either bounded or unbounded domains have been studied in
[
1
,
4
,
5
,
7
,
10
,
11
,
14
,
25
,
33
,
35
,
37
,
40
,
45
,
46
,
48
,
49
,
50
,
59
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
66
,
68
]
. In addition, the long time well-posedness for the water wave problem with small initial data is available in
[
2
,
17
,
24
,
26
,
28
,
60
,
64
,
65
,
69
]
, and there are recent results concerning the life-span for the water wave problem with vorticity
[
18
,
27
,
51
]
.

On the other hand, much less is known for the free-boundary compressible Euler equations, especially for the ones modeling a liquid, as opposed to a gas whose density can be zero on the moving boundary. The LWP for the free-boundary compressible gas model was obtained in
[
9
,
12
,
23
,
29
,
30
,
43
]
, whereas for suitable initial data (e.g., data satisfying the compatibility condition), the LWP for the free-boundary compressible liquid model with
a bounded
fluid domain is available in
[
8
,
13
,
15
,
20
,
36
,
38
,
39
]
.

When the fluid domain is unbounded, the free-boundary compressible Euler equations modeling a liquid is known to be the compressible water wave problem and little is known for this case. The only existence result is due to Trakhinin
[
56
]
, who proved the LWP for the compressible gravity water wave with vorticity using the Nash-Moser iteration (and thus with a loss of regularity). Recently, Luo
[
42
]
established the a priori energy estimates for the compressible gravity water wave with vorticity and proved the incompressible limit by adapting the the approach used in Lindblad-Luo
[
39
]
to an unbounded domain.

The goal of this paper is to prove the LWP for the motion of a compressible gravity water wave without the use of Nash-Moser iteration. The main idea is to approximate the nonlinear compressible water wave problem in the Lagrangian coordinates using a sequence of “tangentially smoothed” problems, whose solutions converge to that of the original problem when the smoothing coefficient goes to
0
. This in the incompressible free-boundary Euler equations goes back to Coutand-Shkoller
[
10
]
. Also, for its application in the compressible free-boundary Euler equations modeling a liquid in a bounded domain, Coutand-Hole-Shkoller
[
8
]
obtained the LWP for the case with surface tension and Ginsberg-Lindblad-Luo
[
20
]
obtained the LWP for the self-gravitating liquid. However, here we use a different set of approximate problems by adapting what appears in
[
20
]
which yields a simpler construction of the sequence of approximate solutions. This will be discussed in Sect.
2.1
.

1.1.1
Difference between a liquid and a gas

This manuscript concerns the compressible Euler equations modeling the motion of
a liquid
, which is treated very differently from
a gas
, as what is studied in
[
29
,
30
]
and
[
43
]
.
The fundamental difference is that the energy for the compressible gas model is weighted by the sound speed
c_{s}(\rho):=\sqrt{p^{\prime}(\rho)}
which vanishes at the physical vacuum boundary (since
\rho
vanishes there), and thus the estimates on the moving boundary are greatly simplified. In the case of a compressible liquid, however, we have to exploit the structure of the equations carefully in order to control the top order terms on the boundary even at the a priori estimate level. Also, it appears that the wave equation verified by the enthalpy
h
plays a crucial role in the construction of a solution. We refer to Section
2.1
for the detailed explanations.

1.2
The Lagrangian coordinates

We introduce the Lagrangian coordinates, under which the moving domain becomes fixed. Let
\Omega:=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(-\infty,0)
to be the lower half space of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Denoting coordinates on
\Omega
by
y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
, we define
\eta:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathcal{D}
to be the flow map of
u
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta(t,y)=u(t,\eta(t,y)),\quad\eta(0,y)=\eta_{0}(y),

(1.12)

where
\eta_{0}:\Omega\to\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a diffeomorphism, satisfying
\|\eta_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}:=\|\partial\eta_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\|\partial^{2}\eta_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}<\infty
(See Notation
1.1
and the remark after (
1.16
) for more details on the choice of this norm). For the sake of simplicity, we assume
\eta_{0}=
Id, i.e., the initial domain is
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(-\infty,0)
. In fact, our approach is also applicable to the case for general data
\eta_{0}
. It is not hard to see that in
(t,y)
coordinates
D_{t}
becomes
\partial_{t}
and the boundary
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
becomes fixed (i.e.,
\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{2}
). We introduce the Lagrangian velocity by
v(t,y):=u(t,\eta(t,y))
, and denote the Lagrangian enthalpy
h(t,y):=h(t,\eta(t,y))
by a slight abuse of notations.

Let
\partial=\partial_{y}
be the spatial derivative in the Lagrangian coordinates. We introduce the matrix
a=(\partial\eta)^{-1}
, specifically
a^{\mu\alpha}=a^{\mu}_{\alpha}:=\frac{\partial y^{\mu}}{\partial\eta^{\alpha}}
. This is well-defined since
\eta(t,\cdot)
is almost an identity map whenever
t
is sufficiently small. In terms of
v,h
and
a
, (
1.6
)-(
1.7
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v^{\alpha}=-\nabla_{a}^{\alpha}h-ge_{3},&~~~\text{in}\,[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\text{div}\,_{a}v=-\partial_{t}e(h),&~~~\text{in}\,[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\eta=\text{Id},v=v_{0},h=h_{0}&~~~\text{on}\,\{0\}\times\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}|_{[0,T]\times\Gamma}\in T([0,T]\times\Gamma)\\
h=0&~~~\text{on}\,\Gamma.\end{cases}

(1.13)

Here, the differential operator
\nabla_{a}:=(\nabla_{a}^{1},\nabla_{a}^{2},\nabla_{a}^{3})
with
\nabla_{a}^{\alpha}=a^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\mu}
denotes the Eulerian (covariant) derivative and
\text{div}\,_{a}v=\nabla_{a}\cdot v=a^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\mu}v_{\alpha}
denotes the Eulerian divergence of
v
. In addition, since
\eta(0,\cdot)=\text{Id}
, we have
a(0,\cdot)=I
, where
I
is the identity matrix, and
u_{0}
and
v_{0}
agree. Furthermore, let
J:=\det(\partial\eta)
. Then
J
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}J=Ja^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\mu}v_{\alpha}.

(1.14)

Finally, we assume the physical sign condition holds initially

\displaystyle-\partial_{3}h_{0}\geq c_{0}>0

(1.15)

and it can be shown that (
1.15
) propagates to a later time.

1.3
The main result

The goal of this paper is to prove the LWP of the compressible gravity water wave system in the Lagrangian coordinates. Specifically, we want to construct a solution to (
1.13
) with localized initial data
(v_{0},h_{0})
, i.e.,
|v_{0}(y)|\to 0
and
|h_{0}(y)|\to 0
as
|y|\to\infty
that satisfies the compatibility condition (
1.11
) up to 4-th order as well as (
1.15
). The localized data is required so that the initial
L^{2}
-based higher order energy functional is bounded and the existence of such data can be found in
[
42
, Section 7]
. Also, we remark here that (
1.15
) remains hold thanks to the presence of the gravity (cf.
[
42
, Section 7]
).

Notation 1.1
.

(
\mathcal{H}
-norm) Let
f
be a smooth function. We define

\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}:=\|\partial f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\|\partial^{2}f\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}.

Definition 1.1
.

We define the higher order energy functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t):=\|\eta(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{4}\|\partial_{t}^{4-k}v(t)\|_{H^{k}(\Omega)}^{2}+\left(\|h(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{3}\|\partial_{t}^{4-k}h(t)\|_{H^{k}(\Omega)}^{2}\right)+|a^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{\alpha}(t)|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2},

(1.16)

where
\overline{\partial}{}=(\partial_{1},\partial_{2})
is the tangential Lagrangian spatial derivative.

Remark
.

The terms
\|\eta\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
and
\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
may be replaced by
\|\eta\|_{H^{4}(\Omega)}^{2}
and
\|h\|_{H^{4}(\Omega)}^{2}
, respectively, in the case when
\Omega
is bounded. However, we have to be more careful in the case of an unbounded
\Omega
since neither
\eta
nor
\partial\eta
are in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, which is due to that
\eta_{0}=\text{Id}
. In addition to this, we cannot control
\partial h
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
due to the presence of the gravity. Because of these, the lower-order terms
\partial\eta
and
\partial h
will be controlled in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
instead.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Main theorem)
Suppose that the initial data
v_{0},h_{0}
satisfies

A.

\|v_{0}\|_{H^{4}(\Omega)},\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq M_{0}
,

B.

the compatibility condition (
1.11
) up to 4-th order, and

C.

the physical sign condition (
1.15
).

Then there exists a
T_{0}>0
and a unique solution
(\eta,v,h)
to (
1.13
) on the time interval
[0,T_{0}]
which satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}\mathcal{E}(t)\leq\mathcal{C}(M_{0}),

(1.17)

where
\mathcal{C}(M_{0})
is a constant depends on
M_{0}
. Also, let
(\hat{v}_{0},\hat{h}_{0})
be another set of initial data satisfying conditions A, B, C, and

\|v_{0}-\hat{v}_{0}\|_{H^{4}(\Omega)},\quad\|h_{0}-\hat{h}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\epsilon_{0}.

Let
(\hat{\eta},\hat{v},\hat{h})
be the solution to (
1.13
) with initial data
(\hat{v}_{0},\hat{h}_{0})
. If

\displaystyle[\mathcal{E}](t):=\|\eta(t)-\hat{\eta}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{2-k}(v(t)-\hat{v}(t))\|_{k}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{2-k}(h(t)-\hat{h}(t))\|_{k}^{2}+|a(\eta)^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}{}^{2}(\eta_{\alpha}(t)-\hat{\eta}_{\alpha}(t))|_{0}^{2},

then

\sup_{t\in[0,T_{0}]}\mathcal{[E]}(t)\leq\mathcal{C}(\epsilon_{0}).

(1.18)

Remark
.

In the case with general initial data
\eta_{0}
, we require that
\|\eta_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq M_{0}
.

1.4
Novelty of this result, comparison with existing results, and application to other fluid models

The result presented in this paper addresses the natural question left open in
[
8
]
, namely, the case of an unbounded domain. However, the method in
[
8
]
requires propagating an extra derivative of the flow map which requires an extra one derivative for the initial vorticity. This is caused by differentiating the Euler equations in the Lagrangian coordinates and all derivatives fall on the cofactor matrix. We are able to avoid this by adapting the Alinhac’s good unknowns, which, in turn, satisfy equations with a better structure.
This is due to that these good unknowns tie to the covariant derivatives of the velocity and pressure in the Eulerian coordinates.
We refer to subsection
2.1.2
for the detailed analysis.

In addition, some lower order terms (e.g.,
\partial h
and
\partial\eta
) are no longer in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as opposed to the case with a bounded fluid domain. As a result, our energy functional (
1.16
) has to be chosen carefully so that the aforementioned quantities are merely in
L^{\infty}
. Nevertheless, (
1.16
) reduces to the following energy in the case of a bounded fluid domain:

\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{4}(\Omega)}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{4}\left(\|\partial_{t}^{4-k}v(t)\|_{H^{k}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{4-k}h(t)\|_{H^{k}(\Omega)}^{2}\right)+|a^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{\alpha}(t)|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}.

In fact, our proof also works for the case of a bounded domain, producing a LWP result but
without propagating the extra regularity of the vorticity and the flow map
. Furthermore, we do not need to consider the surface tension to regularize the free surface and then take the zero surface tension limit.

Finally, the method developed in this manuscript can be adapted to study the LWP for the non-isentropic compressible fluids, relativistic fluids, as well as the (inviscid) complex fluids with moving surface boundary. For a non-isentropic fluid, the equation of states depends on both
\rho
and the entropy
\mathfrak{s}
, i.e.,
p=p(\rho,\mathfrak{s})
, where
\mathfrak{s}
verifies
D_{t}\mathfrak{s}=0
in
\mathcal{D}
. The LWP for the free-boundary problem in non-isentropic fluids is proved in
[
56
]
with a loss of derivatives. Unlike the isentropic case, the enthalpy formulation (
1.6
) is no longer available when
\mathfrak{s}
is present. It is, however, possible to avoid the regularity loss by employing our method in the non-isentropic case by studying the new variable
\log(\rho/\bar{\rho}_{0})
instead of
h
. Moreover, we have learned that Ginsberg-Lindblad
[
19
]
have employed a similar method to study a relativistic fluid with free-surface boundary.
On the other hand, the complex fluids, e.g., magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) and elastodynamics, can be regarded as
Euler equations under the influence of various external forces brought by other physical quantities (e.g., the elasticity, the Lorentz force in an electromagnetic field)
.
The presence of such external forces destroys the Cauchy invariance and thus we are
unable
to propagate the extra regularity on the flow map. Nevertheless, the method developed in this manuscript has been adapted to treat the aforementioned complex fluid models by the second author
[
41
,
67
]
.

List of Notations:

•

\Omega:=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(-\infty,0)
and
\Gamma:=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}
.

•

\|\cdot\|_{s},\|\cdot\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}
: We denote
\|f\|_{s}:=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}
and
\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}(\Omega)}
for any function
f(t,y)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Omega
.

•

|\cdot|_{s},|\cdot|_{\dot{H}^{s}}
: We denote
|f|_{s}:=|f(t,\cdot)|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}
and
|f|_{\dot{H}^{s}}=|f(t,\cdot)|_{\dot{H}^{s}(\Gamma)}
for any function
f(t,y)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Gamma
.

•

P(\cdots)
: A generic polynomial in its arguments.

•

\mathcal{P}_{0}
:
\mathcal{P}_{0}=P(\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}})
.

•

[T,f]g:=T(fg)-T(f)g
, and
[T,f,g]:=T(fg)-T(f)g-fT(g)
, where
T
denotes a differential operator or the mollifier and
f,g
are arbitrary functions.

•

\overline{\partial}{},\overline{\Delta}{}
:
\overline{\partial}{}=\partial_{1},\partial_{2}
denotes the tangential derivative and
\overline{\Delta}{}:=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
denotes the tangential Laplacian.

•

(Eulerian spatial derivative, divergence and curl) Let
f
be a smooth function. Then
\nabla^{\alpha}_{a}f:=a^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\mu}f
,
\alpha=1,2,3
.
Let
\mathbf{f}
be a smooth vector field. Then
\text{div }_{a}\mathbf{f}:=a^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{f}_{\alpha}
and
(\text{curl }_{a}\mathbf{f})_{\lambda}:=\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\alpha}a^{\nu\mu}\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{f}^{\alpha}
, where
\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\alpha}
is the sign of
(\lambda\mu\alpha)\in S_{3}.

Acknowledgment:

The authors would like to thank the referee for his/her careful reading and comments on improving this manuscript.

2
Strategy of the proof and some auxiliary results

2.1
An overview of the proof of Theorem
1.2

The compressible water waves with vorticity are treated
very differently from their incompressible and irrotational counterparts
, as one can completely reduce the latter to a system of quasilinear dispersive equations on the moving interface. The strategy that we employed to prove Theorem
1.2
contains three parts:

1.

The a priori energy estimates in certain functional spaces.

2.

A suitable approximate problem which is asymptotically consistent with the a priori estimate.

3.

Construction of solutions to the approximate problem.

These steps are highly nontrivial in the case of a compressible water wave thanks to the nontrivial divergence of the velocity field and the unbounded fluid domain. The rest of this section is devoted to the elaboration of these steps. Also, we assume
e(h)=h
in the rest of this section for the sake of simple exposition. But general
e(h)
will be studied in the later sections.

Notation 2.1
.

The following notations will be used throughout the rest of this manuscript. Let
f(t,y),g(t,y)
be smooth functions on
[0,T]\times\Omega
and
[0,T]\times\Gamma
, respectively. Then we define
\|f\|_{s}:=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}
and
|g|_{s}:=|g(t,\cdot)|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}
.

2.1.1
Construction of the approximate problem: Tangential smoothing

Although the a priori estimate has been established by the first author in
[
42
]
, it is still quite difficult to obtain a local-in-time solution by a direct iteration scheme based on the a priori bounds. The reason is that a loss of tangential derivative necessarily appears in the linearized system. Specifically, if we start the iteration with the trivial solution
(\eta^{(0)},v^{(0)},h^{(0)})=(\text{Id},0,0)
and inductively define
(\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)},h^{(n+1)})
by the following linearized system

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta^{(n+1)}=v^{(n+1)}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v^{(n+1)}=-\nabla_{{a^{(n)}}}h^{(n+1)}-ge_{3}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}_{a^{(n)}}v^{(n+1)}=-\partial_{t}h^{(n+1)}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
h^{(n+1)}=0~~~&\text{ on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)},h^{(n+1)})|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},v_{0},h_{0}),\end{cases}

(2.1)

where
a^{(n)}:=[\partial\eta^{(n)}]^{-1}
, then:

1.

We have to control
\|\partial^{4}v^{(n+1)}\|_{0}
when constructing the solution for the linearized system, which requires the control of
\|\partial^{4}(\nabla_{{a^{(n)}}}h^{(n+1)})\|_{0}
. The elliptic estimate derived in
[
20
]
(i.e., Lemma
2.8
with
f=h^{(n+1)}
and
\tilde{\eta}
replaced by
\eta^{(n)}
) yields

\|\nabla_{a^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{\dot{H}^{4}}\lesssim\|\Delta_{a^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{3}+\|\overline{\partial}{}\partial\eta^{(n)}\|_{3}\|h^{(n+1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}}

However, the term
\|\overline{\partial}{}\partial\eta^{(n)}\|_{3}
on the RHS cannot be controlled.

2.

Also, we need a uniform-in-
n
energy estimate for (
2.1
) in order to get a solution for the original nonlinear problem by passing
n\to\infty
. During this process, we pick up a boundary term that reads

\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h^{(n+1)}\underbrace{\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{\beta}^{(n)}}_{n\text{-th solution}}a^{(n)3\beta}a^{(n)3\alpha}\underbrace{\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta^{(n+1)}_{\alpha}}_{(n+1)\text{-th solution}}\,dS-\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h^{(n+1)}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{\beta}^{(n)}a^{(n)3\beta}a^{(n)3\alpha}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{\gamma}^{(n)}a^{(n)\mu\gamma}\partial_{\mu}v^{(n+1)}\,dS.

It can be seen that the first term no longer contributes to the positive energy term
|a^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{\alpha}|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}
as opposed to what happens to the original problem due to the loss of symmetry. In addition to this, a cancellation structure that is required to control the second term becomes unavailable as well.

In fact, the issues listed above appear also in the study of incompressible Euler equations
[
10
,
11
]
. To overcome this difficulty, Coutand-Shkoller
[
10
]
introduced the tangential smoothing method: Let
\zeta=\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the standard cut-off function such that
\text{Spt }\zeta=\overline{B(0,1)}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2},~~0\leq\zeta\leq 1
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\zeta=1
. The corresponding dilation is

\zeta_{\kappa}(y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\kappa},\frac{y_{2}}{\kappa}\right),~~\kappa>0,

and we define the smoothing operator as

\Lambda_{\kappa}f(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\zeta_{\kappa}(y_{1}-z_{1},y_{2}-z_{2})f(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\,dz_{1}\,dz_{2}.

(2.2)

Let
\tilde{a}:=[\partial\tilde{\eta}]^{-1}
be the smoothed version of
a
with
\tilde{\eta}:=\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta
and define the approximate system by replacing the coefficient
a
with
\tilde{a}
. Under this setting, we introduce the “tangentially-smoothed” approximate system of the compressible water wave system (
1.13
) as follows

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=v+\psi&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v^{\alpha}=-\nabla_{\tilde{a}}^{\alpha}h-ge_{3}&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}_{\tilde{a}}v=-\partial_{t}h&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
h=0&~~~\text{on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta,v,h)|_{\{t=0\}}=(\text{Id},v_{0},h_{0}),\end{cases}

(2.3)

where
\psi=\psi(\eta,v)
is a correction term which solves the half-space Laplace equation

\begin{cases}\Delta\psi=0&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\psi=\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)&~~~\text{on }\Gamma,\end{cases}

(2.4)

where
\overline{\Delta}{}:=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
is the tangential Laplacian operator and
\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}f:=(|\xi|^{-2}\hat{f})^{\vee}
is the inverse of
\overline{\Delta}{}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The index
\beta
ranges from 1 to 3 and
i
ranges from 1 to 2, as stated after (
1.10
). The notation
\mathbb{P}f:=P_{\geq 1}f
denotes the standard Littlewood-Paley projection in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
which removes the low-frequency part, i.e.,
P_{\geq 1}f:=((1-\chi(\xi))\hat{f}(\xi))^{\vee},
where
0\leq\chi(\xi)\leq 1
is a
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
cut-off function which is supported in
\{|\xi|\leq 2\}
and equals to 1 in
\{|\xi|\leq 1\}
. Also, we mention here that the correction term
\psi\to 0
as
\kappa\to 0
.

Remark
.

The Littlewood-Paley projection
\mathbb{P}
is necessary when we apply the elliptic estimates to control
\psi
:

|\psi|_{3.5}=|\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}f|_{3.5}\lesssim|f|_{1.5},

otherwise the low-frequency part of
\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}f
loses control.

In Ginsberg-Lindblad-Luo
[
20
]
, the compressible Euler equations are approximated by a “fully smoothed system”, in the sense that all variables are replaced by their smoothed version. Specifically, they smooth the velocity vector field in the tangential direction first and then obtain the smoothed flow map by integrating it in time (see
[
20
]
Section 4). In this paper, however, we smooth the flow map directly and through this we replace the nonlinear coefficients
a^{\mu\alpha}
by their smoothed version
\tilde{a}^{\mu\alpha}
in (
2.3
). The advantage of our mollification and the correction term is three-fold.

•

The existence of the solution to the approximate system (
2.3
) can be obtained by passing to the limit as
n\to\infty
in a sequence of approximate solutions
\{(\eta^{(n)},v^{(n)},h^{(n)})\}
which are constructed by solving a linearized version of (
2.3
) (see (
2.14
)).

•

We do not need to construct the initial data for each linearized approximate system as what was done in
[
20
]
, because
a|_{t=0}=\tilde{a}|_{t=0}=I
(the identity matrix) implies that the compatibility conditions of the (linearized) approximate problem are the same as the original system.

•

We are allowed to include a correction term
\psi
in the first equation of (
2.3
), which was first introduced by Gu-Wang
[
22
]
. This is crucial in order to eliminate the higher order terms on the boundary when performing the tangential energy estimate, which shall be explained in Section
2.1.3
-
2.1.4
.

2.1.2
Avoiding extra regularity on the flow map: Alinhac’s good unknown method

The crucial part of the a priori estimates for the approximate system (
2.3
) is the estimate for the tangential derivatives. In particular, the top order tangential energy with full spatial derivatives
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}
enters to the highest order. Due to the special structure of the correction term
\psi
on the boundary, it is more convenient to replace
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}
by
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}
. The corresponding energy reads

\displaystyle\underbrace{\|\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}h\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}}_{=\mathcal{E}_{TI}}+\underbrace{|\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\alpha}|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}}_{=\mathcal{E}_{TB}}.

(2.5)

In the control of
\mathcal{E}_{TI}
, it is necessary to deal with the commutator between
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}
and
\nabla_{\tilde{a}}
, namely
[\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta},\tilde{a}^{\mu\alpha}]\partial_{\mu}f
for
f=h
or
v_{\alpha}
. Such commutators contain the higher order term
(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}a^{\mu\alpha})(\partial_{\mu}f)
with
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{a}=\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\partial\eta\times\partial\eta+\cdots
whose
L^{2}(\Omega)
-norm cannot be directly controlled. In Ginsberg-Lindblad-Luo
[
20
]
, such commutators are controlled by adding
\kappa^{2}
-weighted higher order terms to the energy, which corresponds to the fifth order full spatial energy of the wave equation verified by
h
. In particular, the extra regularity of the flow map
\eta
is necessary in
[
20
]
to close the energy of 5-th order wave equation of
h
. However, we can use the Alinhac’s good unknowns method to get rid of the higher regularity requirement for the flow map.

Motivation of Alinhac’s good unknowns

The main idea is to rewrite
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}h)
and
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\cdot v)
as

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}h)=\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\mathbf{H}+C(h),\text{ with }\|\mathbf{H}-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}h\|_{0}+\|\partial_{t}(\mathbf{H}-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}h)\|_{0}+\|C(h)\|_{0}\leq P(\mathcal{E}(t)),

(2.6)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\cdot v)=\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\cdot\mathbf{V}+C(v),\text{ with }\|\mathbf{V}-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}v\|_{0}+\|\partial_{t}(\mathbf{V}-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}v)\|_{0}+\|C(v)\|_{0}\leq P(\mathcal{E}(t)),

(2.7)

where
P
is a generic polynomial.
Here
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{V}
are called the “Alinhac’s good unknowns” of
h
and
v
, respectively. In other words, the Alinhac’s good unknowns allow us to take into account the covariance under the change of coordinates to avoid the extra regularity assumption on the flow map.

Remark
.

For Euler equations (including the water wave system), it is possible to have higher regularity for
\eta
than
v
thanks to the propagation of the extra regularity assumption on the vorticity. However, when modeling complex fluids with free boundary, such as magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) equations, MHD current-vortex sheets, and elastic fluid equations, it is not possible to have
\eta
more regular than
v
.

Derivation of Alinhac’s good unknowns

It remains to derive the precise expressions of the good unknowns
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{V}
, which is recorded in Lemma
3.4
in full details. Here we give a brief explanation on the precise forms of the good unknowns from the perspective of change of variables. In fact, by chain rule, we can rewrite
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)
in terms of covariant derivatives via
\overline{\partial}{}_{i}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}=\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\eta}^{\alpha}}{\partial y^{i}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\eta}^{\alpha}}=\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}
:

\nabla_{\tilde{a}}(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}f)=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\left((\overline{\partial}{}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}})(\overline{\partial}{}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}})(\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}})(\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}})f\right)=\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)+\nabla_{\tilde{a}}(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)+\text{l.o.t.}

It is not difficult to find that, other than
\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}f
, there is another highest order term
-\nabla_{\tilde{a}}(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)
corresponding to the term that all the derivatives fall on
\tilde{\eta}
. Therefore, the essential highest order term in
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)
is indeed the covariant derivative of
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}f-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f
, called the “Alinhac’s good unknown” of
f
with respect to
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}
.

Therefore, one has

\mathbf{V}:=\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}v-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}v,

and

\mathbf{H}:=\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}h-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}h
, satisfying (
2.6
)-(
2.7
) and

\|C(v)\|_{0}\lesssim P(\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}})(\|\partial v\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial^{2}v\|_{2}),\quad\|C(h)\|_{0}\lesssim P(\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}})(\|\partial h\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial^{2}h\|_{2}).

This circumvents a loss of regularity caused by differentiating the equation
\partial_{t}v^{\alpha}=-\nabla_{\tilde{a}}^{\alpha}h-ge_{3}
and all derivative fall on
\tilde{a}
. Such a remarkable observation is due to Alinhac
[
3
]
. In the study of free-surface fluid, it was first implicitly used in the
Q
-tensor energy introduced by Christodoulou-Lindblad
[
7
]
which was later generalized by
[
42
]
. It has also been applied explicitly in
[
22
,
44
,
61
]
. Recently, Ginsberg-Lindblad
[
19
]
have adapted these good unknowns to study the LWP for the free-boundary relativistic Euler equations in a fixed hyperbolic space-time domain.

Interior estimates via the good unknowns

Now
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{H}
satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{V}=-\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\mathbf{H}+\text{error},\quad\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\cdot\mathbf{V}=\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\text{div}_{\tilde{a}}v)+\text{error},\quad\text{in}\,\,\Omega

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}=-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\partial_{3}h\quad\text{on}\,\,\Gamma,

(2.9)

multiplying
\mathbf{V}
through (
2.8
), integrating over
\Omega
and integrating
\nabla_{\tilde{a}}
in
-\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{V}
by parts yield

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega}|\mathbf{V}|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{H}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\text{div}_{\tilde{a}}v)\,dy+\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}\mathbf{V}_{\alpha}dS+\text{error}.

(2.10)

For the first term on the RHS of (
2.10
), invoking the definition of
\mathbf{H}
and the third equation of (
2.3
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{H}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}(\text{div}_{\tilde{a}}v)\,dy=-\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}h)(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\partial_{t}h)\,dy+\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}h)(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\partial_{t}h)\,dy.

Here, the first term is equal to
-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\|\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}h\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
, which contributes to the positive energy term that controls
\|\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}h\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
. The second term can be controlled after considering its time integral (See (
3.88
)-(
3.89
) for the details).
In addition, invoking the definition of
\mathbf{V}
, we have

\|\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\overline{\Delta}{}v\|_{0}^{2}\leq\|\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\overline{\Delta}{}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}v\|_{0}^{2},

and this implies that it suffices to bound
\|\mathbf{V}\|_{0}^{2}
in order to control
\|\overline{\partial}{}^{4}v\|_{0}^{2}
as the last term
\|\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\overline{\Delta}{}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}v\|_{0}^{2}
can be controlled straightforwardly. For details we refer to the proof of Lemma
3.5
.

2.1.3
Crucial cancellation structure on the boundary

The second term on the RHS of (
2.10
) is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\partial_{t}\eta_{\alpha}-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\psi-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}v_{\alpha})\,dS.

(2.11)

By plugging the definition of
\mathbf{V}
and invoking the first equation of (
2.3
) and then “moving” one
\Lambda_{\kappa}
from
\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}
to
\eta_{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle~~~~\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}\left(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\partial_{t}\eta_{\alpha}-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{a}}v_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h|\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\alpha}|^{2}\,dS

\displaystyle~~~~~+\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{3\gamma}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v_{\gamma}\tilde{a}^{i\alpha}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\alpha}\,dS-\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{\gamma}\tilde{a}^{i\gamma}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v_{\alpha}\,dS

\displaystyle~~~~~+\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{3\gamma}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\psi_{\gamma}\tilde{a}^{i\alpha}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\alpha}\,dS+\text{error}.

(2.12)

The higher order terms on the third line are exactly cancelled out for the original problem (i.e.,
\kappa=0
) but we are unable to control them when
\kappa>0
. However, in light of the definition of
\psi
(
2.4
), both of the higher order terms can indeed be cancelled by
-\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\psi\,dS
in (
2.11
) up to lower order terms plus the low-frequency term

\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\left((\text{Id}-\mathbb{P})\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)\right),

which can be controlled by using Bernstein’s inequality (
2.23
) in Lemma
2.5
. The details can be found in Section
3.6.3
.

Remark
.

Alternatively, one may boost interior regularity of the flow map to
H^{4.5}(\Omega)
. This can be done via the Cauchy invariance but one has to assume the initial vorticity
\text{curl }v_{0}\in H^{3.5}(\Omega)
(cf.
[
32
, Section 4.2]
). The correction term here helps us get rid of extra regularity of the flow map.

2.1.4
Discussion on the uniform-in-
\kappa
energy estimate

We have to make sure that our energy estimate is uniform-in-
\kappa
in order to pass the sequence of approximate solutions to a limit as
\kappa\rightarrow 0
, which, in fact, solves the original problem. This depends crucially on the aforementioned cancellation scheme on the boundary, as the terms in the second line of (
2.1.3
) would otherwise contribute to
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta\|_{\dot{H}^{4}(\Gamma)}
and
\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{\dot{H}^{4}(\Gamma)}=\|\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta\|_{\dot{H}^{4}(\Gamma)}
, respectively, which are of
0.5
-derivatives more regular than
v
after moving to the interior. Of course, one may control these terms by “moving
\overline{\partial}{}^{0.5}
to the tangential mollifier”, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta\|_{\dot{H}^{4}(\Gamma)}\lesssim\kappa^{-1/2}\|\eta\|_{\dot{H}^{3.5}(\Gamma)}.

(2.13)

But this fails to be uniform-in-
\kappa
when
\kappa\rightarrow 0
.

Nevertheless, we have to use (
2.13
) to treat the term in the third line of (
2.1.3
) but we can get an extra
\sqrt{\kappa}
owing to the structure of
\psi
and this cancels
\kappa^{-1/2}
out. Specifically,

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{3\gamma}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\psi_{\gamma}\tilde{a}^{i\alpha}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\alpha}\,dS

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle|\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\gamma}\tilde{a}^{i\alpha}|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}|\overline{\partial}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\psi_{\gamma}|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}|\Lambda_{\kappa}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\eta|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}|\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\beta}|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle|\partial_{3}h\tilde{a}^{3\gamma}\tilde{a}^{i\alpha}|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}|\overline{\partial}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\psi_{\gamma}|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}|\tilde{a}^{3\beta}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\beta}|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\left(\kappa^{-1/2}|\eta|_{\dot{H}^{3.5}(\Gamma)}\right).

We employ the Sobolev embeddings
W^{1,4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
H^{0.5}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and the tangential smoothing property (
2.29
) to have
|\overline{\partial}{}\psi|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}P(\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|v\|_{3})
. We refer to (
3.95
)-(
3.96
) for the details.

2.1.5
Discussion on the existence of the approximate system

The approximate system (
2.3
) can be solved by an iteration of the approximate solutions. Specifically, let
(\eta^{(0)},v^{(0)},h^{(0)})=(\eta^{(1)},v^{(1)},h^{(1)})=(\text{Id},0,0)
(i.e., the trivial solution). For each
n\geq 1
, we inductively define
(\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)},h^{(n+1)})
to be the solution of the linearized system of equations

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta^{(n+1)}=v^{(n+1)}+\psi^{(n)}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v^{(n+1)}=-\nabla_{{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}}h^{(n+1)}-ge_{3}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}v^{(n+1)}=-\partial_{t}h^{(n+1)}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
h^{(n+1)}=0~~~&\text{ on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)},h^{(n+1)})|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},v_{0},h_{0}),\end{cases}

(2.14)

Here,
a^{(n)}:=[\partial\eta^{(n)}]^{-1}
,
\tilde{a}^{(n)}:=[\partial\tilde{\eta}^{(n)}]^{-1}
and the correction term
\psi^{(n)}
is determined by (
2.4
) with
(\eta^{(n)},v^{(n)},\tilde{a}^{(n)})
. The existence of
(\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)},h^{(n+1)})
follows from showing that the map
\Xi:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
(defined below) has a fixed point, where the Banach space
\mathbb{X}
define as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{X}=&\bigg\{(\xi,w,\pi):(w,\xi)|_{t=0}=(v_{0},\text{Id}),\\
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}&\left(\|w(t),\partial_{t}\pi(t)\|_{Z^{4}}+\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi(t),\partial_{t}\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi(t)\|_{Z^{3}}+\|\partial_{t}\xi(t)\|_{Z^{3}}+\|\partial^{2}\xi(t)\|_{Z^{2}}+\|\partial\xi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\leq M\bigg\}.\end{aligned}

(2.15)

Here,
Z^{k}
denotes the mixed space-time
L^{2}
-Sobolev norm of order
\leq k
.
The map
\Xi
is given by

\Xi:(\xi,w,\pi)\mapsto(\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)},h^{(n+1)})

where
we define
\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)}
and
h^{(n+1)}
, respectively, by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta^{(n+1)}=

\displaystyle w+\psi^{(n)},\eta^{(n+1)}(0)=\text{Id},

(2.16)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v^{(n+1)}=

\displaystyle-\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi-ge_{3},v^{(n+1)}(0)=v_{0},

(2.17)

\text{and }\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}h^{(n+1)}-\Delta_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}=-\partial_{t}\tilde{a}_{(n)}^{\nu\alpha}\partial_{\nu}v_{\alpha}^{(n+1)}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
h^{(n+1)}=0~~~&\text{ on }\Gamma,\end{cases}\text{ with }(h^{(n+1)},\partial_{t}h^{(n+1)})|_{t=0}=(h_{0},h_{1}).

(2.18)

Remark
.

The quantity
\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi,\partial_{t}\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi\|_{Z^{3}}
can be replaced by
\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi\|_{Z^{4}}
if
\Omega
is bounded. However, we can merely control
\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi
in
L^{\infty}
as it corresponds to
\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}
.

The estimates for
\eta^{(n+1)}
and
v^{(n+1)}
are straightforward since they verify transport equations. However, the estimate for
\|\partial\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{Z^{3}}
requires that of
\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{\dot{H}^{4}}
which cannot be done directly by commuting
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}
through the wave equation, since there is no hope to control the corresponding source term consists
\partial_{t}\tilde{a}_{(n)}^{\nu\alpha}(\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\partial_{\nu}v_{\alpha}^{(n+1)})
in
L^{2}
.

The key observation here is that
\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\partial_{t}\partial_{\nu}v_{\alpha}^{(n+1)}
can in fact be controlled thanks to (
2.17
) and the finiteness of
\|\partial^{4}\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}\pi\|_{0}
, and so there is no problem to control the wave energies by commuting
\mathcal{D}^{3}\partial_{t}
(where
\mathcal{D}=\overline{\partial}{}
or
\partial_{t}
) through the wave equation (
2.18
). Now, the remaining
\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{\dot{H}^{4}}
can be treated using the elliptic estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{\dot{H}^{4}}\lesssim\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{3}+\|\overline{\partial}{}\partial\tilde{\eta}^{(n)}\|_{3}\|h^{(n+1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}},

(2.19)

which indicates that the control of
\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{\dot{H}^{4}}
requires that of
\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{3}
up to the highest order. But this term is under control since (
2.18
) suggests that

\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{3}\leq\|\partial_{t}^{2}h^{(n+1)}\|_{3}+\|\partial_{t}\tilde{a}_{(n)}^{\nu\alpha}\partial_{\nu}v_{\alpha}^{(n+1)}\|_{3},

where the second term is of lower order and the first term can be controlled by invoking the wave energy with
2
time derivatives.

2.2
Auxiliary results

2.2.1
Sobolev inequalities

Lemma 2.2
.

(Kato-Ponce
[
31
]
inequalities)
Let
J=(I-\Delta)^{1/2},~s\geq 0
. Then the following estimates hold:

(1)
\forall s\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s,p_{1}}}\|g\|_{L^{p_{2}}}+\|f\|_{L^{q_{1}}}\|g\|_{W^{s,q_{2}}},

(2.20)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{s,p_{1}}}\|g\|_{L^{p_{2}}}+\|f\|_{L^{q_{1}}}\|g\|_{\dot{W}^{s,q_{2}}},

with
1/2=1/p_{1}+1/p_{2}=1/q_{1}+1/q_{2}
and
2\leq p_{1},q_{2}<\infty
;

(2)
\forall s\geq 1
, we have

\|J^{s}(fg)-(J^{s}f)g-f(J^{s}g)\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{1,p_{1}}}\|g\|_{W^{s-1,q_{2}}}+\|f\|_{W^{s-1,q_{1}}}\|g\|_{W^{1,q_{2}}}

(2.21)

for all the
1<p<p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}<\infty
with
1/p_{1}+1/p_{2}=1/q_{1}+1/q_{2}=1/p
.

Lemma 2.3
.

(Trace lemma for harmonic functions
[
53
, Prop. 5.1.7]
)

Suppose that
s\geq 0.5
and
u
solves the boundary-value problem

\Delta u=0~\text{ in }\Omega\text{ with }u=g~\text{ on }\Gamma

where
g\in H^{s}(\Gamma)
. Then it holds that

|g|_{s}\lesssim\|u\|_{s+0.5}\lesssim|g|_{s}

Lemma 2.4

(
Normal trace lemma
)
.

It holds that for a vector field
X

|\overline{\partial}{}X\cdot N|_{-0.5}\lesssim\|\overline{\partial}{}X\|_{0}+\|\text{div }X\|_{0}

(2.22)

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in H^{0.5}(\partial\Omega)
be a scalar test function, whose bounded extension in
\Omega
is denoted by
\phi\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. Then

\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\partial}{}X\cdot N\varphi=\int_{\Omega}\text{div }(\overline{\partial}{}X\phi)=\int_{\Omega}\overline{\partial}{}X\nabla\phi-\int_{\Omega}\text{div }X\overline{\partial}{}\phi\lesssim(\|\overline{\partial}{}X\|_{0}+\|\text{div }X\|_{0})|\varphi|_{0.5}.

∎

Lemma 2.5
.

(Bernstein-type inequalities)
Let
0\leq\chi(\xi)\leq 1
be a
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
cut-off function which is supported in
\{|\xi|\leq 2\}
and equals to 1 in
\{|\xi|\leq 1\}
. Define the Littlewood-Paley projection
P_{\leq N}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with respect to
\chi
by

P_{\leq N}f:=\left(\chi(\xi/N)\hat{f}(\xi)\right)^{\vee},~~P_{\geq N}f:=\left((1-\chi(\xi/N))\hat{f}(\xi)\right)^{\vee},~~P_{N}f:=\left((\chi(\xi/N)-\chi(2\xi/N))\hat{f}(\xi)\right)^{\vee}.

Then the following inequalities hold

\displaystyle\|P_{\leq N}f\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,d}N^{s}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},~~~\forall s\geq 0;

(2.23)

\displaystyle\|P_{\geq N}f\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,d}\|f\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},~~~\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.24)

Analogous results also hold for
H_{x}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

For the first inequality, we apply Plancherel’s identity to get

\|P_{\leq N}f\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\||\partial|^{s}P_{\leq N}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\||\xi|^{s}\chi(\xi/N)\hat{f}(\xi)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim N^{s}\cdot 1\cdot\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Note that
s\geq 0
is used in the last inequality.
For the second inequality, we just replace
\chi(\xi/N)
above by
1-\chi(\xi/N)
and notice that
0\leq 1-\chi(\xi/N)\leq 1
to get

\|P_{\geq N}f\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\||\xi|^{s}\hat{f}(\xi)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Analogous results hold for
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by replacing
|\partial|
and
|\xi|
with
\langle\partial\rangle
and
\langle\xi\rangle
respectively. One can see Tao
[
52
, Appendix A]
for more Bernstein-type inequalities.
∎

2.2.2
Properties of tangential smoothing operator

As stated in the introduction, we are going to use the tangential smoothing to construct the approximate solutions. Here we list the definition and basic properties which are repeatedly used in this paper. Let
\zeta=\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a standard cut-off function such that
\text{Spt }\zeta=\overline{B(0,1)}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2},~~0\leq\zeta\leq 1
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\zeta=1
. The corresponding dilation is

\zeta_{\kappa}(y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\kappa},\frac{y_{2}}{\kappa}\right),~~\kappa>0.

Now we define

\Lambda_{\kappa}f(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\zeta_{\kappa}(y_{1}-z_{1},y_{2}-z_{2})f(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\,dz_{1}\,dz_{2}.

(2.25)

The following lemma records the basic properties of tangential smoothing.

Lemma 2.6
.

(Regularity and Commutator estimates)
For
\kappa>0
, we have

(1) The following regularity estimates:

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f\|_{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{s},~~\forall s\geq 0;

(2.26)

\displaystyle|\Lambda_{\kappa}f|_{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim|f|_{s},~~\forall s\geq-0.5;

(2.27)

\displaystyle|\overline{\partial}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}f|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa^{-s}|f|_{1-s},~~\forall s\in[0,1];

(2.28)

\displaystyle|f-\Lambda_{\kappa}f|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}|\overline{\partial}{}f|_{0.5}.

(2.29)

(2) Commutator estimates: Define the commutator
[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]g:=\Lambda_{\kappa}(fg)-f\Lambda_{\kappa}(g)
. Then it satisfies

\displaystyle|[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]g|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim|f|_{L^{\infty}}|g|_{0},

(2.30)

\displaystyle|[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]\overline{\partial}{}g|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim|f|_{W^{1,\infty}}|g|_{0},

(2.31)

\displaystyle|[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]\overline{\partial}{}g|_{0.5}

\displaystyle\lesssim|f|_{W^{1,\infty}}|g|_{0.5}.

(2.32)

Proof.

(1): The estimates (
2.27
) and (
2.28
) follows directly from the definition (
2.25
) and the basic properties of convolution. (
2.29
) is derived by using Sobolev embedding and Hölder’s inequality:

\displaystyle|f-\Lambda_{\kappa}f|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\cap B(0,\kappa)}\zeta_{\kappa}(z)(f(y-z)-f(y))\,dz\right|\lesssim\left|\zeta_{\kappa}\right|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}}}\left|\kappa\overline{\partial}{}f\right|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\left|\zeta\right|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}}}\left|\overline{\partial}{}f\right|_{0.5}.

(2): The first three estimates can be found in
[
11
, Lemma 5.1]
. To prove the fourth one, we note that

\overline{\partial}{}([\Lambda_{\kappa},f]g)=\Lambda_{\kappa}(\overline{\partial}{}f\overline{\partial}{}g)+\Lambda_{\kappa}(f\overline{\partial}{}^{2}g)-\overline{\partial}{}f\Lambda_{\kappa}\overline{\partial}{}g-f\Lambda_{\kappa}\overline{\partial}{}^{2}g=[\Lambda_{\kappa},\overline{\partial}{}f]\overline{\partial}{}g+[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]\overline{\partial}{}^{2}g.

From (
2.30
) and (
2.31
) we know

|\overline{\partial}{}[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]g|_{0}\lesssim|\overline{\partial}{}f|_{L^{\infty}}|\overline{\partial}{}g|_{0}+|f|_{W^{1,\infty}}|\overline{\partial}{}g|_{0}\lesssim|f|_{W^{1,\infty}}|g|_{1}.

(2.33)

Therefore (
2.32
) follows from the interpolation of (
2.31
) and (
2.33
).
∎

2.2.3
Elliptic estimates

Lemma 2.7
.

(Hodge-type decomposition)
Let
X
be a smooth vector field and
s\geq 1
, then it holds that

\|X\|_{s}\lesssim\|X\|_{0}+\|\text{curl }X\|_{s-1}+\|\text{div }X\|_{s-1}+|X\cdot N|_{s-0.5}.

(2.34)

Proof.

This follows from the well-known identity
-\Delta X=\text{curl }\text{curl }X-\nabla\text{div }X
and integration by parts.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

(Interior elliptic estimate)

The following elliptic estimate holds for
f=0
on
\Gamma
.

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle C(\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{H}})\left(\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{0}^{2}+\|\partial f\|_{0}^{2}\right),\quad\text{or}\quad\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq C(\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{H}})\left(\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{0}^{2}+\|\partial f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\right),

(2.35)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{r}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle C(\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{H}})\left(\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{r-1}^{2}+\|\partial f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\partial^{2}f\|_{r-2}^{2}\right),\quad r=2,3,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{r}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle C(\|\partial\tilde{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\tilde{\eta}\|_{r-2})\Bigg(\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{r-1}^{2}+\|\overline{\partial}{}\partial\tilde{\eta}\|_{r-1}^{2}\left(\|\partial f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\partial^{2}f\|_{r-2}^{2}\right)\Bigg),\quad r\geq 4.

Proof.

The proof is largely similar to what is in
[
20
, Appendix B]
and so we shall only sketch the details. The main idea here is to apply the div-curl estimate on
\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{r}}^{2}
. The (Eulerian) divergence contributes to the Laplacian term, and the (Eulerian) curl of
\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f
vanishes. Then the term
\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{0}^{2}
will be generated by Lemma
2.4
during this process. To control this term, we write

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)=\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f)+\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)([\overline{\partial}{}^{r},\nabla_{\tilde{a}}]f),

where

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)([\overline{\partial}{}^{r},\nabla_{\tilde{a}}]f)\lesssim\epsilon\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{0}^{2}+\|[\overline{\partial}{}^{r},\nabla_{\tilde{a}}]f\|_{0}^{2},

and

\|[\overline{\partial}{}^{r},\nabla_{\tilde{a}}]f\|_{0}^{2}
is controlled by either
C(\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{H}})\|\partial f\|_{1}^{2}
or
C(\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{H}})\|\partial f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}
when
r=1
, by
C(\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{H}})\left(\|\partial f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\partial^{2}f\|_{r-2}^{2}\right)
when
r=2,3
, and by
C(\|\partial\tilde{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\tilde{\eta}\|_{r-2})\|\overline{\partial}{}\partial\tilde{\eta}\|_{r-1}^{2}\left(\|\partial f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\partial^{2}f\|_{r-2}^{2}\right)
when
r\geq 4
. Moreover, by integrating
\nabla_{\tilde{a}}
by parts, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f)=-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f),

(2.36)

and there is no boundary term since
f=0
on
\Gamma
implies
\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f=0
on
\Gamma
. The main term contributed by the RHS of (
2.36
) after commuting
\nabla_{\tilde{a}}
through
\overline{\partial}{}^{r}
is
-\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f)
, which can be controlled by integrating
\overline{\partial}{}
by parts and then using the
\epsilon
-Young’s inequality, i.e.,

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f)=\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r-1}\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r+1}f)\lesssim\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{r-1}^{2}+\epsilon\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r+1}f\|_{0}^{2},

where
\epsilon\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r+1}f\|_{0}^{2}\leq\epsilon\|\partial\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f\|_{0}^{2}
, and
\epsilon\|\partial\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f\|_{0}^{2}
is comparable to
\epsilon\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{0}^{2}
modulo error terms that take the form
\|[\overline{\partial}{}^{r},\nabla_{\tilde{a}}]f\|_{0}^{2}
. On the other hand, the error term generated by the RHS of (
2.36
) after commuting
\nabla_{\tilde{a}}
through
\overline{\partial}{}^{r}
takes the form

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}[\overline{\partial}{}^{r},\tilde{a}^{\mu\alpha}]\partial_{\mu}(\tilde{a}^{\nu}_{\,\,\alpha}\partial_{\nu}f)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f),

and it contributes to (up to the highest order)

I:=\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\tilde{a}^{\mu\alpha})\left(\partial_{\mu}(\tilde{a}^{\nu}_{\,\,\alpha}\partial_{\nu}f)\right)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f),\quad\text{and}\quad II:=\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}\tilde{a}^{\mu\alpha})\left(\overline{\partial}{}^{r-1}\partial_{\mu}(\tilde{a}^{\nu}_{\,\,\alpha}\partial_{\nu}f)\right)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f).

Here,

\displaystyle II\lesssim\epsilon\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{r}}^{2}+\|(\overline{\partial}{}\tilde{a})(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f)\|_{0}^{2},

and
\|(\overline{\partial}{}\tilde{a})(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f)\|_{0}^{2}
can be bounded directly by the RHS of (
2.35
).
Also, for
I
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}\tilde{a}^{\mu\alpha})\left(\partial_{\mu}(\tilde{a}^{\nu}_{\,\,\alpha}\partial_{\nu}f)\right)(\overline{\partial}{}f)\lesssim\epsilon\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}+\|(\overline{\partial}{}\tilde{a})(\overline{\partial}{}f)\|_{0}^{2},

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\tilde{a}^{\mu\alpha})\left(\partial_{\mu}(\tilde{a}^{\nu}_{\,\,\alpha}\partial_{\nu}f)\right)(\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f)\lesssim\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f\|_{0}^{2}+\|\partial(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\tilde{a}\|_{0}^{2},\quad\text{when}\,\,r\geq 2,

where
\|(\overline{\partial}{}\tilde{a})(\overline{\partial}{}f)\|_{0}^{2},\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r}f\|_{0}^{2}
and
\|\partial(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{r}\tilde{a}\|_{0}^{2}
can all be bounded directly by the RHS of (
2.35
).
∎

Remark
.

The inequalities in (
2.35
) can be simplified to

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{r}}^{2}\leq C(\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{\mathcal{H}})\left(\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{r-1}^{2}+\|f\|_{r}^{2}\right),\quad r=1,2,3,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{\dot{H}^{r}}^{2}\leq C(\|\partial\tilde{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\tilde{\eta}\|_{r-2})\Bigg(\|\Delta_{\tilde{a}}f\|_{r-1}^{2}+\|\overline{\partial}{}\partial\tilde{\eta}\|_{r-1}^{2}\|f\|_{r}^{2}\Bigg),\quad r\geq 4,

when
\Omega
is a bounded domain. Nevertheless, for an unbounded domain
\Omega
we have to be more careful when
f=h
since
\partial h
can only be controlled in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

3
The Approximate system and uniform a priori estimates

In this section we are going to introduce the approximation of the water wave problem and derive its uniform a priori estimates.

3.1
The approximate system

For
\kappa>0
, we consider the following approximate system

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=v+\psi&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v=-\nabla_{\tilde{a}}h-ge_{3}&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}_{\tilde{a}}v=-e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}h&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
h=0&~~~\text{on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta,v,h)|_{\{t=0\}}=(\text{Id},v_{0},h_{0}).\end{cases}

(3.1)

Here
\tilde{a}:=(\partial\tilde{\eta})^{-1}
where
\tilde{\eta}
is the smoothed version of the flow map
\eta
defined by
\tilde{\eta}:=\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta
. The term
\psi=\psi(\eta,v)
is a correction term which solves the half-space Laplacian equation

\begin{cases}\Delta\psi=0&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\psi=\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)&~~~\text{on }\Gamma,\end{cases}

(3.2)

where
\mathbb{P}f:=P_{\geq 1}f
denotes the standard Littlewood-Paley projection in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined is Lemma
2.5
, which removes the low-frequency part.
\overline{\Delta}{}:=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
denotes the tangential Laplacian operator and
\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}f:=(-|\xi|^{-2}\hat{f})^{\vee}
is the inverse of
\overline{\Delta}{}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Remark
.

1.

The correction term
\psi\to 0
as
\kappa\to 0
. We introduce such a term to eliminate the higher order boundary terms which appears in the tangential estimates of
v
. These higher order boundary terms are zero when
\kappa=0
but cannot be controlled when
\kappa>0
.

2.

The Littlewood-Paley projection is necessary here because we will repeatedly use

|\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}f|_{s}\lesssim|\mathbb{P}f|_{{H}^{s-2}}\approx|\mathbb{P}f|_{\dot{H}^{s-2}}\lesssim|f|_{\dot{H}^{s-2}},

which can be proved via Bernstein inequality (
2.24
). Without
\mathbb{P}
the low-frequency part loses control when taking
\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}.

Fix any
\kappa>0
, we will prove in Section
4
that there exists a
T_{\kappa}>0
depending on the initial data and
\kappa>0
such that there is a unique solution
(v(\kappa),h(\kappa),\eta(\kappa))
to (
3.1
) in
[0,T_{\kappa}]
. For simplicity we omit the
\kappa
and only write
v,h,\eta
in this manuscript. The remaining context in this section is to derive the uniform-in-
\kappa
a priori estimates for the solutions to (
3.1
).
This guarantees that we are able to obtain the solution of the original problem in some fixed time interval by passing
\kappa\to 0
.

For simplicity in notations, we still denote the solution to the
\kappa
-approximation system by
(\eta,v,h)
with
\kappa
omitted. Define the energy functional for the
\kappa
-approximate problem (
3.1
) to be

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(t):=\|\eta(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{4}\|\partial_{t}^{4-k}v(t)\|_{k}^{2}+\left(\|h(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{3}\|\partial_{t}^{4-k}h(t)\|_{k}^{2}\right)+|\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\alpha}(t)|_{0}^{2}.

(3.3)

Remark
.

We recall that (
3.3
) can be simplified to

\|\eta(t)\|_{4}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{4}(\|\partial_{t}^{4-k}v(t)\|_{k}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{4-k}h(t)\|_{k}^{2})+|\tilde{a}^{3\alpha}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{\alpha}(t)|_{0}^{2}.

in the case a bounded domain. We refer to the remark after (
1.16
) and Section
1.4
for the details.

The rest of this section is devoted to prove:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
be defined as above. Then there exists a time
T>0
independent of
\kappa
such that

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(t)\leq P(\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}).

(3.4)

Proposition
3.1
is a direct consequence of the following proposition:

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
be defined as above. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(t)\leq P(\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}})+\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\tau))\,d\tau,\quad\forall t\in[0,T]

(3.5)

provided the following a priori assumptions hold

\displaystyle-\partial_{3}h(t)\geq\frac{c_{0}}{2}

\displaystyle~~~\text{on }\Gamma,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{J}(t)-1\|_{3}\leq\epsilon

\displaystyle~~~\text{in }\Omega,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\|\text{Id}-\tilde{a}(t)\|_{3}\leq\epsilon

\displaystyle~~~\text{in }\Omega,

(3.8)

where
\tilde{J}:=\det(\partial\tilde{\eta})
and we use
\epsilon>0
to denote the sufficiently small number which appears here and the
\epsilon
-Young inequality.

Remark
.

It suffices to show that (
3.5
) holds true when
t=T
.
Also, (
3.5
) can in fact be reduced to

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(T)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}P(\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(t))\,dt.

(3.9)

In
[
42
]
we are able to prove that there exists initial data satisfying the compatibility condition (
1.11
) up to order
5
such that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(0)\leq P(\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}})
holds. For notation simplicity we define
\mathcal{P}_{0}:=P(\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}})
.

3.2
Estimates for the flow map and correction term

First we bound the flow map and the correction term together with their smoothed version by the quantities in
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
. The following estimates will be repeatedly use in this section.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(v,h,\eta)
be the solution to (
3.1
). Then we have

\displaystyle\|\partial\tilde{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},

(3.10)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{2}\tilde{\eta}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{3}),

(3.12)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\psi\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3}),

(3.13)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}),

(3.14)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2},\|\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1}).

(3.15)

and

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\eta}\|_{4}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4}),

(3.16)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{\eta}\|_{3}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{2}\eta\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3}),

(3.17)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{3}\tilde{\eta}\|_{2}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{3}\eta\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}),

(3.18)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{4}\tilde{\eta}\|_{1}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{4}\eta\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2},\|\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1}).

(3.19)

Proof.

First, (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) follow from (
2.26
), i.e.,
\|\partial\tilde{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}
,
\|\partial^{2}\tilde{\eta}\|_{2}=\|\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2}\lesssim\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2}
. To bound
\partial_{t}^{k}\tilde{\eta}
, it suffices to bound the same norm of
\partial_{t}^{k}\eta
and then apply (
2.26
) again. From the first equation of (
3.1
), one has
\partial_{t}^{k+1}\eta=\partial_{t}^{k}v+\partial_{t}^{k}\psi
, so the estimates (
3.16
)-(
3.19
) automatically holds once we prove (
3.12
)-(
3.15
).

Commuting time derivatives through (
3.2
), we get the equations for
\partial_{t}^{k}\psi~(k=0,1,2,3,4)
:

\begin{cases}\Delta\partial_{t}^{k}\psi=0&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}^{k}\psi=\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}\partial_{t}^{k}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)&~~~\text{on }\Gamma.\end{cases}

(3.20)

By the standard elliptic estimates, Sobolev trace lemma and Bernstein inequality (
2.24
) in Lemma
2.5
, we can get

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)\right|_{3.5}

(3.21)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right|_{1.5}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2}\|\tilde{a}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{3}\leq P(\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|v\|_{3}).

Also, when
k=1,2,3
, one has

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\psi\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\partial_{t}\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)\right|_{3.5}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\partial_{t}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)\right|_{1.5}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3}),

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\partial_{t}^{2}\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)\right|_{2.5}

(3.23)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\partial_{t}^{2}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)\right|_{0.5}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{2}(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}),

and

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\partial_{t}^{3}\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)\right|_{1.5}

(3.24)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|~\mathbb{P}\partial_{t}^{3}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)\right|_{-0.5}

where in the last step we apply the Bernstein’s inequality (
2.24
).

Combining with
\partial_{t}^{k+1}\eta=\partial_{t}^{k}v+\partial_{t}^{k}\psi
, (
3.16
), (
3.17
) and (
3.18
) directly follows from (
3.22
) and (
3.23
), respectively. When
k=3
, one has to be cautious because the leading order term in (
3.24
) is of the form
(\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\Delta}{}\eta)\tilde{a}\overline{\partial}{}v
and
\overline{\Delta}{}\eta\tilde{a}(\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\partial}{}v)
which can only be bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)
by the quantites in
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
and thus loses control on the boundary. To control these terms on the boundary, we have to use the fact that
\dot{H}^{0.5}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=(\dot{H}^{0.5}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))^{*}
.

First we separate them from other lower order terms which has
L^{2}(\Gamma)
control.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\partial_{t}^{3}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)

(3.25)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\underbrace{\left(\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v+\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}v\right)}_{\text{leading order terms=:X}}+\mathbb{P}Y.

The control of
Y
is straightforward by using Sobolev trace lemma and (
3.16
), (
3.17
),

\displaystyle|\mathbb{P}Y|_{-0.5}

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbb{P}Y|_{0.5}\lesssim\|Y\|_{1}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial_{t}^{2}\eta\|_{2.5},\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{3.5},\|\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{a}\|_{1.5},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{1.5},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{2.5})

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}).

As for the
|\mathbb{P}X|_{-0.5}
term, we first use the Bernstein inequality (
2.24
) to get
|\mathbb{P}X|_{-0.5}\approx|\mathbb{P}X|_{\dot{H}^{-0.5}}\lesssim|X|_{\dot{H}^{-0.5}}
. Then the duality between
\dot{H}^{-0.5}
and
\dot{H}^{0.5}
yields that for any test function
\phi\in\dot{H}^{0.5}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
|\phi|_{\dot{H}^{0.5}}\leq 1
, one has

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v,\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v,\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\phi\rangle

(3.27)

\displaystyle=\langle\overline{\partial}{}_{i}^{0.5}\partial_{t}^{3}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v,\overline{\partial}{}_{i}^{0.5}(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{\beta}\tilde{a}^{i\beta}\phi)\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim|\partial_{t}^{3}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v|_{\dot{H}^{0.5}}|\overline{\Delta}{}\eta\tilde{a}\phi|_{\dot{H}^{0.5}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1}(|\phi|_{\dot{H}^{0.5}}|\overline{\Delta}{}\eta\tilde{a}|_{L^{\infty}}+|\overline{\Delta}{}\eta\tilde{a}|_{\dot{W}^{0.5,4}}|\phi|_{L^{4}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1}(\|\partial^{2}\eta\|_{2}\|a\|_{L^{\infty}})|\phi|_{\dot{H}^{0.5}}.

Here we integrate 1/2-order tangential derivative on
\Gamma
by part in the second step, and then apply trace lemma to control
|\partial_{t}^{3}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v|_{\dot{H}^{0.5}}
and Kato-Ponce product estimate (
2.20
) to bound
|\overline{\Delta}{}\eta\tilde{a}\phi|_{\dot{H}^{0.5}}
